[w39e3fd-1] pmc Cancer Med Cancer Med 1974 canmed 101595310 CAM4 Cancer Medicine 2045-7634 Wiley PMC13684316 PMC13684316.1 13684316 13684316 42902936 10.1002/cam4.72393 CAM472393 5125668 1 Research Article Research Article Incidence, Mortality, and Survival Trends of Liver Cancer in the Canton of Zurich, Switzerland, Between 1980 and 2022 Zuber Nina 1 Suter Flurina https://orcid.org/0000-0002-5302-2268 1 2 Wanner Miriam https://orcid.org/0000-0003-0888-0690 1 Korol Dimitri https://orcid.org/0000-0002-9217-8038 1 Rohrmann Sabine https://orcid.org/0000-0002-2215-1200 1 2 sabine.rohrmann@uzh.ch 1 Cancer Registry of the Canton Zurich, Zug, Schaffhausen and Schwyz University Hospital Zurich Zurich Switzerland
[w39e3fd-2] 2 Division of Chronic Disease Epidemiology, Epidemiology, Biostatistics, and Prevention Institute University of Zurich Zurich Switzerland * Correspondence: Sabine Rohrmann ( sabine.rohrmann@uzh.ch )
[w39e3fd-3] 21 10 2026 10 2026 15 10 522303 10.1002/cam4.v15.10 e72393 16 9 2026 08 5 2026 10 10 2026 21 10 2026 22 10 2026 22 10 2026 © 2026 The Author(s). Cancer Medicine published by John Wiley & Sons Ltd. https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/ This is an open access article under the terms of the http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/ License, which permits use, distribution and reproduction in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited. ABSTRACT Background The global burden of liver cancer has been increasing over recent decades, whereas the incidence in Switzerland has remained relatively stable. This study assessed long‐term trends in incidence, mortality, and 1‐ and 5‐year relative survival of primary liver cancer in the Swiss canton of Zurich from 1980 to 2022, stratified by sex and age.
[w39e3fd-4] Methods This population‐based retrospective analysis included all primary liver cancer cases diagnosed in the canton of Zurich and registered in the Cancer Registry of the cantons of Zurich, Zug, Schaffhausen and Schwyz between 1980 and 2022. Mortality data were obtained from the Federal Statistical Office. Cases were classified according to ICD‐O‐3 topography and morphology and, from 2003 onward, TNM stage. Age‐standardised incidence and mortality rates were calculated using the 1976 European standard population. Temporal trends were analysed using Joinpoint regression and reported as Average Annual Percentage Changes (AAPCs) with 95% confidence intervals (CIs). Relative survival was estimated using the Ederer II method and standardised according to international standards.
[w39e3fd-5] Results Among 3866 incident cases, 71.3% were hepatocellular carcinomas and 70.7% occurred in men. Overall age‐standardised incidence remained stable in both men (AAPC −0.1%, 95% CI −0.6 to 0.3%) and women (−0.2%, −0.9% to 0.4%). However, incidence increased significantly among individuals younger than 65 years, particularly in women (AAPC 2.3%, 1.3%–3.3%) and men (1.2%, 0.6%–1.9%). Mortality remained broadly stable in men and increased modestly in women. One‐ and five‐year relative survival improved substantially, from 13.8% and 3.5% in 1980–1999 to 53.9% and 25.8% in 2016–2022, respectively. Improvements were greater in men than women.
[w39e3fd-6] Conclusion These findings reflect major advances in liver cancer diagnosis and treatment over the past four decades. Continued prevention, targeted screening of high‐risk groups, and epidemiological monitoring remain important. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY source-schema-version-number 2.0 cover-date October 2026 details-of-publishers-convertor Converter:WILEY_ML3GV2_TO_JATSPMC version:6.8.9 mode:remove_FC converted:21.10.2026 1 Introduction Liver cancer continues to pose a significant threat to global health, with projections indicating over one million new cases per year by 2040 [ 1 ]. Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) represents the predominant form of this malignancy, comprising approximately 90% of all liver cancer diagnoses [ 2 ]. Established risk factors for HCC include chronic hepatitis B and C infections, alcohol consumption, steatotic liver disease (SLD), and exposure to certain toxins such as aflatoxin [ 3 ]. The relative contribution of these risk factors varies substantially according to geographical region and population characteristics [ 4 ]. In recent years, the nomenclature of fatty liver disease has been revised. According to the 2023 multisociety Delphi consensus, SLD is the overarching term, while metabolic dysfunction‐associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) replaces the former term non‐alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD), and metabolic dysfunction‐associated steatohepatitis (MASH) replaces non‐alcoholic steatohepatitis (NASH) [ 5 ]. MASLD is defined by the presence of hepatic steatosis together with at least one cardiometabolic risk factor. Epidemiologically, contrasting trends are observed: While HCC incidence is declining in some Asian countries due to successful hepatitis B vaccination programmes, it is rising in many parts of the world, including the United States, Australia, and most parts of Europe, driven by the increasing prevalence of non‐alcoholic fatty liver disease [ 1 , 6 ].
[w39e3fd-7] Liver cancer generally has low survival [ 7 ]. One reason for the poor prognosis is that it is often diagnosed at a more advanced stage. In early stages of the disease, surgical removal is the standard treatment. Only 5%–15% of all liver cancer patients are diagnosed at an early stage and therefore qualify for surgical resection. For more advanced stages, treatment options include trans‐arterial chemoembolization and chemotherapeutics [ 8 ].
[w39e3fd-8] According to the Global Cancer Statistics 2022, liver cancer ranks as the sixth most commonly diagnosed cancer worldwide, accounting for 4.3% of all new cancer cases, and the third leading cause of cancer‐related deaths, responsible for 7.8% of all such deaths [ 9 ]. In Switzerland, liver cancer accounted on average for 11.6% (men) and 3.6% (women) of all newly diagnosed cancer cases in Switzerland in the years 2017–2021. Meanwhile, liver cancer accounted for 7.8% of all cancer‐related deaths among men and 2.8% among women [ 10 ].
[w39e3fd-9] The aim of this study was to analyse incidence, mortality, one‐year and five‐year relative survival of liver cancer in the Swiss canton of Zurich between 1980 and 2022. 2 Methods The study followed the outline of the Reporting of studies Conducted using Observational Routinely collected Data (RECORD) guidelines [ 11 ].
[w39e3fd-10] 2.1 Data Sources, Inclusion Criteria and Study Design We performed a retrospective analysis using data for the canton of Zurich from the Cancer Registry of the cantons of Zurich, Zug, Schaffhausen, and Schwyz (CRZZSS) and the Federal Statistical Office (FSO). The CRZZSS contains data on liver cancer patients residing in the canton of Zurich from 1980 to 2022. Death statistics for liver cancer are available at the FSO.
[w39e3fd-11] Since 1980, the cantonal cancer registry has systematically collected and documented data on all cancer cases diagnosed among residents of the canton of Zurich at the time of diagnosis. The registry ensures a comprehensive recording of newly diagnosed cancer cases within the population for epidemiological monitoring and cancer research purposes. Reporting of such cases is mandatory by law since 2020 and involves the submission of detailed clinical and pathological information by healthcare providers, while adhering to strict confidentiality and data protection regulations. Before 2020, the registration was based on a decision of the Zurich Government Council from 1980 and the Cantonal Cancer Registration Law from 2017. The registry's work supports the evaluation of cancer incidence, treatment outcomes, and the effectiveness of preventive measures within the canton. This organised collection and analysis of data is aligned with national standards under the Swiss Cancer Registration Act to ensure consistency and quality in cancer surveillance [ 12 , 13 , 14 ].
[w39e3fd-12] The incidence analysis was based on primary liver cancer cases (C22.0 and C22.1 according to the International Classification of Diseases for Oncology, Third Edition (ICD‐O‐3) [ 15 ]) diagnosed in the canton of Zurich between 1980 and 2022. Cases were excluded if the date of birth was missing, if a death certificate was the only source of information, or if they were lost to follow‐up.
[w39e3fd-13] Liver cancer cases were categorised into five histological subgroups based on the ICD‐O‐3 [ 15 ] morphology codes: HCC (8170, 8171, 8172, 8174); intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma (iCCA) (8140, 8160, 8161, 8560, 8576); combined hepatocellular and intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma (8180); other specified histology (8010, 8020, 8033, 8041, 8162, 8240, 8246, 8840, 8890, 8910, 8970, 8991, 9120, 9130, 9133); and unspecified histology (8000).
[w39e3fd-14] Liver cancer cases were staged according to the TNM classification of malignant tumours (TNM) (I, II, III, IV or ‘missing’). Staging analysis included cases diagnosed from 2003 to 2022, as systematic tumour stage documentation was not available prior to 2003. TNM editions were assigned by year of diagnosis: sixth edition (TNM6) for 2003–2009; seventh edition (TNM7) for 2010–2017; and eighth edition (TNM8) for 2018–2022. Where pathological metastasis (pM) data were unavailable, clinical data (cM) were used, and vice versa; this approach was applied consistently to node and tumour data (pN, cN, pT, cT). When both clinical and pathological data were available for node, metastasis, or tumour, but discordant, pathological data were prioritised for analysis. In cases where neoadjuvant therapy was administered, clinical TNM was used.
[w39e3fd-15] Histological subgroups and staging were used only for incident case characterisation. Incidence and survival trends for these subgroups were not analysed because the number of cases within each subgroup was limited. The data included information regarding sex of the patient (male, female), year of diagnosis (1980–1999, 2000–2015, 2016–2022), age at diagnosis (< 65 years, 65–74 years, ≥ 75 years), topography (C22.0, C22.1) and morphology (HCC, iCCA, cHCCiCCA, unspecified, other specified) according to ICD‐O‐3, basis of diagnosis, vital status at last follow‐up (alive, dead, lost to follow‐up), year of last follow‐up, age at last follow‐up, and administration of neoadjuvant therapy (yes, no).
[w39e3fd-16] The mortality data used from the FSO included all registered deaths in the canton of Zurich for the years 1980–2022. The dataset contained the year of birth, year of death, sex, and the main cause of death. The main cause of death was coded according to the International Classification of Diseases, 8th revision (ICD‐8) [ 16 ] up to 1994 and according to the 10th revision (ICD‐10) from 1995 onward. For the mortality analysis, all cases with ICD codes C22.0, C22.1 and 155 were included.
[w39e3fd-17] 2.2 Statistical Analysis Characteristics of incident liver cancer cases and liver cancer‐related deaths were analysed descriptively. Categorical variables were displayed as absolute numbers and percentages, overall and stratified by sex. Age‐standardised incidence and mortality rates per 100,000 person‐years were calculated using the 1976 European standard population [ 17 ] and population estimates from the canton of Zurich. Due to the unavailability of population data for 1980, population data from 1981 were used instead. Incidence and mortality rates for liver cancer were determined overall and stratified by sex, age group, and period of diagnosis or death.
[w39e3fd-18] Statistical analyses were conducted using R version 4.5.2 [ 18 ]. Temporal trends in incidence and mortality were assessed with Joinpoint Trend Analysis Software (Version 5.4.0.0 [ 19 ]). The Joinpoint model initially assumed zero joinpoints (indicating a single linear trend), and additional joinpoints were tested sequentially and retained only if they significantly improved the model fit, assessed by permutation tests with Bonferroni‐adjusted p ‐values to compare model fits [ 20 ]. Following the Joinpoint software manual recommendations [ 21 ], the maximum number of joinpoints was limited to five. A minimum of two observations was required between each joinpoint and at the dataset's boundaries, including when a joinpoint coincided with an observation.
[w39e3fd-19] The dependent variables were age‐standardised incidence and mortality rates, with calendar year as the independent variable. The models estimated annual percentage changes (APC) for each linear trend segment and the average annual percentage change (AAPC) as a summary measure for the entire observation period (1980–2022). Because the data were not normally distributed, dependent variables were log‐transformed. AAPC estimates are reported with corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CI) [ 22 ].
[w39e3fd-20] Survival time was defined as age at death or last‐follow‐up, whichever date occurred first, minus the age at diagnosis. Age‐standardised one‐year and five‐year relative survival were estimated as the ratio of observed to expected survival, irrespective of the cause of death. Expected survival was derived using the Ederer II method, based on lifetables. Relative survival estimates were age‐standardised according to the International Cancer Survival Standards [ 23 ], applying standard weights for the following age groups: 0.07 (0–44 years), 0.12 (45–54 years), 0.23 (55–64 years), 0.29 (65–74 years), and 0.29 (≥ 75 years).
[w39e3fd-21] 3 Results Between 1980 and 2022, a total of 4091 incident liver cancer cases were recorded in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. Of these, two cases were excluded due to missing birth date information, 210 cases were excluded because a death certificate was the only source of information, and 13 cases were lost to follow‐up. In total, 3866 incident liver cancer cases met the inclusion criteria for the present study. During the same period, 3381 liver cancer deaths were registered. Tables 1 and 2 describe the cases by sex, diagnostic period, age at diagnosis, topography code, histological subgroup, and cancer stage for incidence and by sex, period of death, and age at death for mortality.

[w39e3fd-25] 3.1 Incidence Between 1980 and 1999, the overall age‐standardised incidence of liver cancer was 7.4 per 100,000 person‐years in men and 2.8 in women. In the most recent period (2016–2022), the corresponding rates were 8.6 in men and 3.7 in women. Over the entire observation period from 1980 to 2022, the AAPC was −0.1% (95% CI −0.6% to 0.3%) among men and −0.2% (95% CI −0.9% to 0.4%) among women, indicating that the overall incidence of liver cancer remained largely stable over time. Age‐standardised incidence rates were consistently higher in men than in women across all periods (Table 3 , Figure 1 ).

[w39e3fd-29] Joinpoint regression analysis identified two significant joinpoints for both sexes. In men, a significant decline in incidence rates was observed up to 1999, followed by a significant increase until 2002, and a subsequent stable trend. Among women, the trend showed a significant decrease until 2000, a significant increase until 2003, and a non‐significant decline in the following years (Table 4 , Figure 1 ). Despite these temporal fluctuations, long‐term trends suggest relatively stable age‐standardised incidence rates over the four‐decade study period. [w39e3fd-32] The age‐specific analysis showed that up to about 40 years of age, the incidence of liver cancer was very low but increased thereafter and was highest in individuals aged 70 years and older (Figure 2 ). The analyses stratified by sex and age group (Figure 3 ) underline these findings with the highest incidence rate observed in the oldest age group (≥ 75 years) in most years. FIGURE 2 Age‐standardised incidence rates for liver cancer (per 100,000 person‐years) according to five‐year age groups, by sex and period, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. The observed values (points) are connected by a line.
[w39e3fd-33] FIGURE 3 Trends in age‐standardised incidence rates for liver cancer (per 100,000 person‐years) from 1980 to 2022, by sex and age group, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. The points show the observed values, and the linear trend line indicates the average percentage change. Jointpoint regression analysis stratified by age group revealed a significant increase in age‐standardised liver cancer incidence among individuals younger than 65 years throughout the entire study period. The AAPC was 1.2% (95% CI 0.6%–1.9%) for men and 2.3% (95% CI 1.3&–3.3%) for women (Figure 3 ).
[w39e3fd-34] 3.2 Mortality Between 1980 and 1999, age‐standardised liver cancer mortality rate was 6.8 per 100,000 person‐years in men and 2.0 in women. In the most recent period (2016–2022), rates were 6.0 and 2.7, respectively. Over the full observation period (1980–2022), the AAPC was 0.7% (95% CI −0.6% to 1.6%) in men and 1.4% (95% CI 0.7%–2.1%) in women, suggesting a stable trend in men and a modest increase in women. Mortality rates consistently remained higher in men than in women throughout the whole study period (Table 3 , Figure 4 ).
[w39e3fd-35] FIGURE 4 Trends in age‐standardised mortality rates for liver cancer (per 100,000 person‐years) from 1980 to 2022, by sex, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. The points show the observed values, and the linear trend line indicates the average percentage change. Joinpoint regression analysis identified one joinpoint in men, indicating a significant increase until 1983 followed by a subsequent decline, whereas no joinpoint was detected in women, in whom mortality increased significantly and consistently over the study period (Table 4 , Figure 4 ).
[w39e3fd-36] In both sexes, liver cancer mortality was very low up to the age of about 40 years, but increased rapidly thereafter (Figure 5 ). The analyses stratified by sex and age group (Figure 6 ) underline the findings, with the highest mortality rates observed in the oldest age group (≥ 75 years) and the lowest mortality rates in the youngest age group (< 65 years) throughout the whole study period. FIGURE 5 Age‐standardised mortality rates for liver cancer (per 100,000 person‐years) according to five‐year age groups, by sex and period, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. The observed values (points) are connected by a line.
[w39e3fd-37] FIGURE 6 Trends in age‐standardised mortality rates for liver cancer (per 100,000 person‐years) from 1980 to 2022, by sex and age group, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. The points show the observed values, and the linear trend line indicates the average percentage change.
[w39e3fd-38] 3.3 One‐ and Five‐Year Relative Survival Age‐standardised one‐year and five‐year relative survival for liver cancer improved markedly over the study period. Overall, one‐year relative survival increased from 13.8% (95% CI 12.0%–15.9%) in 1980–1999 to 40.0% (95% CI 37.5%–42.5%) in 2000–2015 and 53.9% (95% CI 50.6%–57.4%) in 2016–2022. Five‐year relative survival increased from 3.5% (95% CI 2.5%–4.8%) to 16.2% (95% CI 14.3%–18.5%) and 25.8% (95% CI 22.5%–29.6%) across the same periods (Table 3 ).
[w39e3fd-39] The observed improvement was higher in men than in women. Among men, 1‐year relative survival increased from 13.0% (95% CI 10.9%–15.4%) in 1980–1999 to 56.0% (95% CI 51.9%–60.3%) in 2016–2022, while 5‐year relative survival increased from 2.5% (95% CI 1.6%–3.9%) to 27.9% (95% CI 23.8%–32.8%). Among women, 1‐year relative survival increased from 16.5% (95% CI 13.1%–20.8%) to 49.9% (95% CI 44.4%–56.1%), and 5‐year relative survival from 6.0% (95% CI 3.9%–9.2%) to 21.9% (95% CI 17.0%–28.2%).
[w39e3fd-40] 4 Discussion This study analysed long‐term epidemiological trends in primary liver cancer cases in the canton of Zurich over more than four decades. Between 1980 and 2022, around 4000 incident liver cancer cases were documented—predominantly hepatocellular carcinoma—alongside almost 3400 deaths due to liver cancer. The proportion of HCCs in our cohort was 71.3%, which is lower than the approximately 90% generally reported for primary liver cancers. This difference is likely attributable, at least in part, to differences in local morphology coding. In particular, the relatively high proportion of cases classified as unspecified (5.5%) and the comparatively high proportion of intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma (iCCA; 19.3%) may contribute to the observed discrepancy and should therefore not necessarily be interpreted as a contradiction.
[w39e3fd-41] Throughout the study period, incidence and mortality rates were consistently higher in men than women. Notably, incidence rates increased among individuals younger than 65 years, while mortality rates rose significantly among women only. One‐ and five‐year relative survival improved markedly over time, reflecting gradual progress in early detection and treatment outcomes.
[w39e3fd-42] 4.1 Comparison With Other Studies 4.1.1 National Perspective (Switzerland) According to the data from the National Institute for Cancer Epidemiology and Registration (NICER), incidence and mortality rates of primary liver cancer in Switzerland increased only modestly between 1980 and 2010. NICER also reported progressive improvements in short‐term survival. Between 1990 and 1999, the age‐standardised relative one‐ and three‐year survival rates reached 27.9% and 11.8% in men and 29.8% and 11.6% in women, respectively; these improved to 40.2% and 19.9% in men and 42.3% and 19.4% in women between 2000 and 2009. These national figures confirm the gradual improvement in survival outcomes observed in Zurich [ 24 ].
[w39e3fd-43] 4.1.2 Regional Comparison (Europe) Across Europe, marked variation exists in liver cancer incidence and mortality. In 2020, age‐standardised incidence rates ranged from 4.3 per 100,000 person‐years in Central Eastern Europe to 5.4 in Western and 6.7 in Southern Europe. Western European countries such as France, Spain, and Italy—previously those with the highest incidence—reported lower rates in 2019 compared to 2010, whereas modest increases were observed in Northern Europe. The Global Burden of Disease study attributed these diverging trends to shifts in aetiology, with alcohol‐related liver disease and metabolic dysfunction‐associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) increasingly contributing to the liver cancer burden, while viral hepatitis‐related liver cancer has declined in several regions following vaccination and antiviral therapies [ 25 ].
[w39e3fd-44] Italy, historically characterised by high hepatitis C virus (HCV) prevalence, has shown a decline in liver cancer incidence following widespread direct‐acting antivirals use [ 26 ], while Nordic and Central European countries may face future increases in liver cancer burden related to the growing prevalence of obesity, metabolic dysfunction, and MASLD [ 27 ].
[w39e3fd-45] This broader European trend is supported by a population‐based Dutch study investigating hepato‐pancreato‐biliary cancers between 1989 and 2022. The study reported a steady rise in liver cancer incidence—from 3.0 to 8.2 per 100,000 person‐years in men and from 1.2 to 3.9 in women—mirrored by concurrent increases in mortality. Relative 5‐year survival improved markedly from 3%–6% to around 18%–21%, consistent with the positive survival development seen in our Zurich cohort and across Western Europe [ 28 ].
[w39e3fd-46] In France, a comprehensive analysis of patients diagnosed with liver cancer between 2009 and 2012 revealed a median survival of 9.4 months (95% CI 9.2–9.8 months) post‐diagnosis, with actuarial survival rates of 45.4% at 12 months, 22.8% at 36 months, and 9.6% at 60 months [ 29 ].
[w39e3fd-47] A follow‐up study analysing trends in survival of primary liver cancer in the United Kingdom between 2000 and 2021 reported survival patterns comparable to those in our study. One‐year survival increased from 35.6% (95% CI 30.5%–41.5%) in 2000–2004 to 42.7% (36.9%–49.4%) in 2020–2021. Among men, 1 year‐survival rose from 33.2% (27.2%–40.4%) to 45.5% (38.8%–53.4%), whereas in women it declined from 40.6% (31.7%–51.9%) to 34.6% (24.3%–49.3%). Similarly, five‐year survival increased from 12.0% (8.7%–16.6%) in 2000–2004 to 15.6% (13.0%–18.8%) in 2015–2019. In men, five‐year survival improved from 9.6% (6.1%–15.0%) to 16.1% (13.1%–19.8%), while in women it decreased from 17.2% (10.8%–27.4%) to 14.6% (9.9%–21.8%) [ 30 ].
[w39e3fd-48] 4.1.3 Global Trends At the international level, the Global Burden of Disease analyses have consistently demonstrated pronounced geographic variation in primary liver cancer incidence and mortality. Between 1990 and 2016, global age‐standardised incidence increased from 18.4 to 22.3 per 100,000 person‐years in men and from 7.2 to 7.5 in women [ 31 ]. The 2020 update estimated a global incidence rate of 9.5 per 100,000 person‐years with the highest burden in Eastern Asia (17.8 per 100,000 person‐years) and the lowest in South‐Central Asia (3.0 per 100,000 person‐years). Corresponding global mortality was 8.7 per 100,000 person‐years, with highest rates in Eastern Asia (16.1 per 100,000 person‐years) and lowest rates in South‐Central Asia (2.8 per 100,000 person‐years) [ 1 ].
[w39e3fd-49] More recent estimates from 2022 refined this picture reporting 866,136 new liver cancer cases worldwide with men accounting for nearly 70% of diagnoses. The global age‐standardised incidence rate was 8.6 per 100,000 person‐years overall (12.7 in men and 4.8 in women). The global mortality burden remained similarly high, with 758,725 deaths and an age‐standardised mortality rate of 7.4 per 100,000 person‐years (10.9 in men and 4.1 in women). Nearly half of all new cases and deaths occurred in East Asia, emphasising the substantial regional disparities in liver cancer burden. The highest age‐standardised incidence rates were reported in Micronesia (15.1 per 100,000 person‐years), Eastern Asia (14.7), and Northern Africa (14.2), while the highest age‐standardised mortality rates occurred in Northern Africa (13.7), followed by Southeastern (13.0), and Eastern Asia (11.9) [ 32 ]. Prognosis remains poor, with the maximum relative five‐year survival estimated at 18% in high‐income countries. These global observations underline that men are much more affected by liver cancer than women and highlight geographical variations based on differences in risk factors [ 6 ].
[w39e3fd-50] 4.2 Interpretations The higher incidence rates of liver cancer observed in men compared to women are not yet fully understood. Both biological and behavioural explanations are plausible. Women may benefit from a protective effect of oestrogen and are less likely to smoke or consume alcohol—two well‐established risk factors for liver cancer [ 33 ].
[w39e3fd-51] The observed increase in liver cancer mortality among women despite relatively stable overall incidence warrants particular attention. Several factors may potentially contribute to this pattern, including differences in disease stage at diagnosis, tumour biology, underlying liver disease, treatment allocation, or access to and receipt of cancer‐directed therapies. Differences in diagnostic pathways may also play a role, including the possibility of delayed diagnosis or differences in the detection of asymptomatic disease. However, these explanations cannot be evaluated directly in the present study because individual‐level information on disease aetiology, detailed tumour characteristics, treatment allocation, comorbidities, and access to care was not available. The observed sex‐specific mortality pattern should therefore be interpreted as an epidemiological finding rather than evidence for a specific underlying mechanism.
[w39e3fd-52] The rising incidence among individuals under 65 years of age might reflect changes in the prevalence of metabolic and behavioural risk factors, including obesity, diabetes, smoking and alcohol consumption, particularly in younger populations [ 34 ]. The increasing prevalence of metabolic dysfunction and MASLD may therefore represent one potential contributor to these trends. However, because individual‐level data on metabolic risk factors, alcohol consumption, and liver disease aetiology were not available in our registry dataset, the relative contribution of MASLD, MetALD, alcohol‐related liver disease, and other risk factors to the observed increase cannot be determined. In contrast, the incidence of hepatitis B and C infections has declined substantially in Switzerland due to the successful vaccination and preventive measures, and the availability of new antiviral therapies that effectively prevent the chronic progression of these infections [ 35 , 36 ]. Because chronic HBV and HCV infections often lead to liver cancer many years after infection, the decrease in HBV and HCV infections may have contributed towards stable rather than rising liver cancer incidence rates in older age groups [ 35 , 36 ].
[w39e3fd-53] An additional consideration under the updated SLD nomenclature is metabolic dysfunction‐associated steatotic liver disease with increased alcohol intake (MetALD). MetALD recognises the substantial overlap between metabolic dysfunction and alcohol exposure, which may be particularly relevant in Western populations where obesity, metabolic risk factors, and alcohol consumption frequently coexist [ 5 ]. This overlap may complicate the attribution of liver cancer to a single underlying aetiology and suggest that some cases previously classified as NAFLD or alcohol‐related liver disease may now fall within the MetALD spectrum. Given that our registry data did not contain individual‐level information on metabolic risk factors or alcohol consumption, we were unable to directly assess the contribution of MASLD or MetALD to the observed liver cancer trends.
[w39e3fd-54] The improved survival rates observed in Switzerland and other countries may reflect a combination of advances in diagnostic imaging, earlier detection, more individualised treatment strategies, and the availability of novel therapeutic approaches. Progress in systemic therapies, including the introduction of sorafenib after 2007 and atezolizumab‐bevacizumab after 2020, as well as advances in liver transplantation and locoregional treatments, may have contributed to improved outcomes. However, individual‐level treatment data and longitudinal information on treatment eligibility and access were not available for our cohort, and the distribution of treatment modalities across periods could therefore not be assessed. Consequently, the contribution of specific therapeutic advances to the observed survival improvement cannot be determined. In addition, broader use and improved sensitivity of cross‐sectional imaging may have resulted in earlier diagnosis and lead‐time bias or stage‐migration, while changes in case ascertainment and an increasing proportion of incidentally detected or asymptomatic small HCCs may also have contributed to the observed survival trends [ 8 , 37 ].
[w39e3fd-55] 4.3 Strengths and Limitations Major strengths of this study include the long observation period and the high quality of cancer registry data [ 38 ]. In addition, stratified analyses by sex, age group, and study period enabled the detection of subgroup‐specific trends, while the use of age‐standardised rates allowed for meaningful comparisons across time, between studies, and countries. Nonetheless, several limitations merit consideration. The number of cases per incidence year remains relatively small, limiting the precision of some subgroup estimates. This is particularly relevant for the earliest study period (1980–1999), in which the low annual case counts may have resulted in less precise and less stable 5‐year relative survival estimates. These early survival estimates should therefore be interpreted with caution. Additionally, as the analysis was restricted to data from the canton of Zurich, the generalisability of these findings to other regions may be limited.
[w39e3fd-56] Furthermore, the registry data did not include individual‐level information on metabolic risk factors, alcohol consumption, or underlying liver disease aetiology. Consequently, the contribution of MASLD, MetALD, alcohol‐related liver disease, and other specific aetiologies to the observed temporal trends could not be directly quantified.
[w39e3fd-57] An important limitation of this study concerns the assessment of disease stage. TNM staging was used because it was the staging system available in the population‐based cancer registry. For HCC, however, the Barcelona Clinic Liver Cancer (BCLC) system [ 39 ] is the established clinical framework for prognostic classification and treatment allocation, as it incorporates tumour burden together with liver function and performance status. BCLC staging could not be reliably reconstructed in our cohort because key variables required for its assignment, particularly Child‐Pugh or ALBI grade and performance status, were not routinely recorded in the registry.
[w39e3fd-58] Furthermore, TNM stage was missing for 58.8% of cases diagnosed between 2003 and 2022, despite TNM staging being systematically recorded during this period. This substantial proportion of missing staging information represents an important limitation and restricts the interpretations of stage distributions and stage‐specific temporal trends. The reasons for missing TNM stage could not be determined from the registry data; therefore, missingness cannot be assumed to be random, and systematic differences in stage ascertainment across study periods or clinical settings cannot be excluded. Consequently, changes in TNM stage distribution should be interpreted cautiously and should not be considered a complete representation of changes in disease severity over time. In particular, TNM‐based staging should not be interpreted as a substitute for BCLC classification in HCC.
[w39e3fd-59] Another limitation concerns the comparability of mortality data over the study period due to the transition from ICD‐8 to ICD‐10 in 1995. Mortality cases were defined using ICD‐8 code up to 1994 and ICD‐10 codes C22.0 and C22.1 from 1995 onward. Although these codes were used consistently with the mortality case definition applied in the study, changes in coding practices associated with the transition between classification systems may affect comparability across the entire observation period and could potentially introduce a discontinuity around 1995. Therefore, long‐term mortality trends should be interpreted with some caution around the coding transition. No additional sensitivity analysis was performed.
[w39e3fd-60] Finally, the most recent study period includes the years 2020–2022, which coincided with the COVID‐19 pandemic. The pandemic may have affected healthcare utilisation, diagnostic pathways, cancer detection, treatment delivery, and cancer registration, potentially influencing incidence and survival estimates during this period. Evidence from Swiss cancer registry data supports this concern. Suter et al. reported a decrease in cancer diagnoses during the first year of the pandemic, particularly during the most stringent lockdown phase in April 2020, with some cancer incidence rates increasing again in 2021. In a subsequent analysis, they also observed differences in cancer stage distribution and longer times to treatment initiation for some treatment modalities among patients diagnosed during the pandemic compared with the pre‐pandemic period. These findings suggest that disruptions in cancer detection and treatment may have affected cancer outcomes during the pandemic period. However, the extent to which such effects influenced the estimates in the present cannot be quantified because individual‐level information on pandemic‐related delays in diagnosis or treatment was not available. Moreover, changes observed in Swiss cancer registry data during this period may also partly reflect the implementation of the new Swiss Cancer Registration Act in 2020. Therefore, estimates for 2020–2022 should be interpreted with some caution, particularly when considering short‐term changes at the end of the observation period [ 40 , 41 ].
[w39e3fd-61] 5 Conclusions This long‐term analysis revealed a rising incidence of liver cancer among individuals younger than 65 years of age and an increasing mortality rate among women between 1980 and 2022. These trends may reflect changes in several established risk factors, including metabolic dysfunction and MASLD, alcohol consumption, and chronic viral hepatitis infections. However, because detailed information on these risk factors was not available, their respective contributions to the observed trends could not be directly assessed.
[w39e3fd-62] Strengthening primary prevention efforts remains essential to counter these trends. Public health initiatives targeting obesity reduction, promotion of moderate alcohol consumption, and the expansion of hepatitis B vaccination coverage could lower future liver cancer incidence. Broader implementation of screening programmes for high‐risk groups—such as individuals with chronic liver disease—would further support earlier diagnosis and improve treatment outcomes.
[w39e3fd-63] Encouragingly, 1‐ and 5‐year relative survival for liver cancer have improved steadily over the past decades. This progress may reflect advances in diagnostic imaging, earlier detection, wider availability of liver transplantation, locoregional treatments, and systemic therapies, including molecularly targeted agents and immunotherapy. However, because detailed treatment data were unavailable, the contribution of specific therapeutic advances to the observed survival improvement cannot be determined. Moreover, multidisciplinary management integrating hepatology and oncology expertise appears to have optimised patient care and improved clinical outcomes. Ongoing research should continue to evaluate how evolving therapeutic approaches and preventive strategies can further reduce the burden of liver cancer in Switzerland and beyond. Continued monitoring of national trends will be essential to assess the effectiveness of prevention strategies and therapeutic innovations.

[w39e3fd-65] Ethics Statement In the Canton of Zurich, all cancer cases are registered with presumed consent and registered based on a decision from 1980 by the Zurich Government Council and the general registry approval from 1995 by the Federal Commission of Experts for professional secrecy in medical research. In this analysis, all data were used anonymously, and therefore, no approval was required from the Ethics Committee of the Canton of Zurich. Conflicts of Interest The authors declare no conflicts of interest.

It is distributed in accordance with the Creative Commons Attribution NonCommercial (CC BY-NC 4.0) license, which permits others to distribute, remix, adapt, build upon this work non-commercially, and license their derivative works on different terms, provided the original work is properly cited and the use is non-commercial. See: https://creativecommons.org/Licenses/by-nc/4.0/ Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) is an oncofetal glycoprotein that has been used as a tumor marker for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) in combination with ultrasound and other imaging modalities. Its utility is limited because of both low sensitivity and specificity, and discrepancies among the different methods of measurements. Moreover, its accuracy varies according to patient characteristics and the AFP cut-off values used. Combination of AFP with novel biomarkers such as AFP-L3, Golgi specific membrane protein (GP73) and des-gamma-carboxyprothrombin significantly improved its accuracy in detecting HCC. Increased AFP level could also signify severity of hepatic destruction and subsequent regeneration and is commonly observed in patients with acute and chronic liver conditions and cirrhosis. Hereditary and other non-hepatic disorders can also cause AFP elevation.

[w6567c2-5] Core Tip: Alpha-fetoprotein has been used commonly as a biomarker for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) surveillance. Its sensitivity and specificity can be affected by the assay methods, patient characteristics and severity of the underlying liver conditions. Combination with other novel markers has shown promising results. Algorithms integrating these serum markers with noninvasive diagnostic imaging modalities are essential for the accurate and timely diagnosis of HCC.
[w6567c2-6] INTRODUCTION During fetal and neonatal life, alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) is produced by yolk sac, fetal liver and gastrointestinal tract. Only a trace amount of AFP can be measured in adults due to its rapid decline in adults[ 1 - 4 ]. The use of AFP as a tumor marker for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) was first proposed in 1960s. The utility of AFP as a surveillance and diagnostic test has been criticized due to both low sensitivity and specificity. AFP, however, continues to play a significant role for HCC surveillance in combination with ultrasound (US) and other imaging modalities[ 5 - 8 ]. This review focused on both the clinical roles and limitations of AFP. In addition, the patterns of AFP elevation in various non-HCC etiologies were discussed.
[w6567c2-7] BIOLOGY OF AFP AFP is an oncofetal glycoprotein consisting of a single polypeptide chain of approximately 600 amino acids and nearly 4% carbohydrate. With molecular weight of 67500 dalton, it is a negatively charged protein with an isoelectric point of pH 4.57 and displays several charge isomers by extended agarose gel electrophoresis[ 1 , 2 ]. During the first trimester, AFP is mainly produced by yolk sac. After the atresia of yolk sac by 11-12 wk, fetal liver becomes the predominant source of AFP. A trace amount is also produced by the gastrointestinal tract of the fetus[ 1 ]. AFP in fetal serum can be detected as early as 29 d after conception and reaches its peak value of 3.0 × 10 6 ng/mL by 14 th week of gestation. It declines to 2.0 × 10 5 –3.0 × 10 5 ng/mL by week 32 and further decreases to 20-120 ng/mL at term[ 3 ]. By six months of life, serum AFP level is approximately 30 ng/mL. Thereafter, it is detectable between 3 and 15 ng/mL in adulthood[ 3 , 4 ].
[w6567c2-8] AFP belongs to the family of serum albumin and the genes of which are present on the chromosome 4. It shares structure and physiochemical properties with its family members namely albumin, vitamin D-binding protein and afamin[ 9 ]. As both albumin and AFP show significant amino acid homologies, AFP is suggested to be an embryonic analogue of albumin[ 10 ]. Furthermore, because of its similarity to albumin, it has been postulated that AFP could be a carrier protein. In addition, its role as an immune-regulator and as a carrier protein for bilirubin has also been suggested[ 11 ].
[w6567c2-9] AFP, being a principal serum binding protein in the fetus, plays a vital role in carrying various ligands such as fatty acids, hormones, minerals and bilirubin[ 12 ]. Though it performs many physiological functions during fetal development, elevated levels in adults are frequently observed in liver carcinogenesis and various disease processes[ 13 - 15 ]. Many biologic studies support the pro-oncogenic as well as anti-apoptotic effects of AFP. AFP can stimulate cell proliferation, cell motility and invasive properties of HCC cell lines and there is evidence that apoptosis of cancer cells could be achieved by blocking AFP[ 16 , 17 ]. On the other hand, a number of cancerous and non- cancerous causes involving both the liver and other organs can also lead to AFP elevation. AFP, therefore, is a non-specific marker of HCC[ 7 ] (Table 1 ).
[w6567c2-10] Table 1 Causes of alpha-fetoprotein elevation[ 13 - 15 ] Hepatic Non-hepatic Neoplastic Neoplastic Hepatocellular carcinoma Germ cell tumors (testicular and ovarian malignancies) Intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma Gastric cancer Non-neoplastic Non-neoplastic Liver cirrhosis Normal pregnancy/infancy Fulminant acute hepatitis Colitis Acute and chronic viral hepatitis Fetal disorders (Gastroschisis, Neural tube defect) Chronic liver diseases Ataxia telangiectasia Biliary obstruction (Intrahepatic and extrahepatic causes) Hereditary tyrosinemia type 1 Drug induced hepatitis Hereditary AFP persistence Alcohol liver disease Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome
[w6567c2-11] Non-alcoholic liver disease Systemic lupus erythematosus Neonatal hepatitis Hirschsprung’s disease Massive hepatic necrosis Wilson disease Hemochromatosis AFP: Alpha-fetoprotein.
[w6567c2-12] HOW IS AFP MEASURED? AFP was originally measured by immunoelectophoresis, but this method was not very sensitive. In the 1970s and 1980s, new techniques such as radioimmunoassay and enzyme immunoassays were used. Subsequently, a quantitative automated chemiluminescent enzyme immunoassay was developed, which replaced and refined the previous clinical assays[ 18 , 19 ]. In this method, the serum sample is placed on a magnetic plate which is already bound by an anti-AFP antibody. A second chemiluminescent detection antibody, added to the same magnetic plate, then binds to all the AFP that is present in excess. All the unbound detection antibody is washed off and an organic substrate called as developer is added which emits light and become luminescent (Figure 1A ). A chemiluminometer is used to detect the antibody, and quantification of the results is done against the known AFP standards. However, measurement interference may occur. In a single step method of AFP detection, sometimes interfering antibodies would bind to both the capture and detect antibodies leading to a false positive result (Figure 1B ). Conversely, the interfering antibodies may blind to the reagents and inhibiting the proper interaction of AFP with the specific anti-AFP antibodies[ 20 ] (Figure 1C ).
[w6567c2-13] Figure 1 ELISA measures. A: ELISA measures alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) by detecting reactivity with two anti-AFP antibodies; B: Interfering antibodies that bind directly to the capture and detect reagents without the target AFP (no analytes). This binding would emit light and become luminescent causing false positive results and high values; C: Interfering antibodies can inhibit reactivity of the ELISA by binding to the reagents and prevent the detection of AFP by the anti-AFP antibodies; that leads to a false negative result. AFP: Alpha-fetoprotein.
[w6567c2-14] AFP as a diagnostic marker for HCC The association between AFP and HCC is well recognized; however, the sensitivity and specificity of the AFP assay varies according to patient characteristics, design of the study, and the AFP cut-off values used[ 21 , 22 ] (Table 2 ). A systematic review of studies using AFP at a threshold value of 20 ng/mL in patients with cirrhosis, the sensitivity and specificity of detecting HCC were 41%-65% and 80%-94%, respectively[ 22 ]. Lowering the cut-off can increase the sensitivity of AFP but at the cost of higher false positives. By increasing the AFP value from 20 to 50 ng/mL, the specificity increased to 96% with a positive predictive value of 75% but sensitivity was reduced to 47%[ 23 ]. AFP values also vary according to the tumor size. The correlation of AFP to the size of the tumor was evaluated by Saffroy et al [ 24 ] . The bigger tumors generally had higher AFP values. The sensitivity of AFP, therefore, decreased from 52% for HCC > 3 cm to 25% for those < 3 cm in diameter[ 24 ].
[w6567c2-15] Table 2 Sensitivity and specificity of alpha-fetoprotein at different cut-off values[ 22 - 24 ] AFP cut-off value Sensitivity Specificity 20 Approximately 60% 90% 50 47% 96% 100 31.2% 98.8% 400 17% 99.4% AFP: Alpha-fetoprotein.
[w6567c2-16] For AFP-producing HCC, AFP plays an important role in initiating monitoring and terminating HCC therapy. A complete response of the treatment can be expected if preceding levels of AFP decrease to normal on follow ups. AFP, however, cannot be applied to monitor treatment response if its levels were not elevated prior to treatment[ 25 - 27 ]. Approximately one-third of the patients with HCC have no AFP elevation[ 28 ]. Nomura et al [ 29 ] characterized those HCCs with low or no elevation of AFP. These patients generally have favorable prognosis with low probability of HCC recurrence and improved survival as compared to those with elevated AFP levels[ 29 , 30 ]. In a large retrospective study including 1800 patients with HCC, 42% had AFP < 20 ng/mL whereas 16% had AFP levels between 20 and 100 ng/mL. Thus, a total of 58% had AFP < 100 ng/mL. The authors observed that 67% of patients with HCC smaller than 5 cm had AFP ≤ 100 ng/mL vs only 49% of the patients with HCC larger than 5 cm had AFP ≤ 100 ng/mL. In addition, HCCs without AFP elevation (≤ 100 ng/mL) were less likely to develop portal vein thrombosis[ 31 ].
[w6567c2-17] AFP-L3 and other HCC biomarkers Based on the binding capacity of AFP to lens culinaris agglutinin (LCA), three separated bands of AFP glycoforms can be identified with the Western blotting. These are AFP-L1 (non-binding), AFP-L2 (intermediate binding) and AFP-L3 (LCA-reactive). AFP-L1 generally correlates with hepatic inflammation in chronic hepatitis while AFP-L3 is more specific for HCC. AFP-L2 is derived from yolk sac and is detectable in the maternal serum during pregnancy[ 32 ] (Table 3 ). Table 3 Overview of the diagnostic parameters for hepatocellular carcinoma Diagnostic parameter (cut off) Remark AUROC AFP (> 20 ng/mL) Sensitivity: 40%–65% 0.54–0.80 Specificity: 76%–96%
[w6567c2-18] AFP-L3 (> 15%) Sensitivity: 45%–90% 0.74–0.84 Specificity: 95% AFP-L3 + AFP-P4 + P5 (> 15% for both biomarkers) Sensitivity: 55.3%–61% 0.76 Specificity: 82.3%–93.9% AFP, AFP-L3 and DCP (3-25 ng/mL, 1%-10%, 0.48 ng/mL or 40 mAu/mL) Sensitivity: 81%–93% 0.88–0.93 Specificity: 69%-87% AFP and GP73 Sensitivity: 75%-91% 0.91–0.95 Specificity: 81%-97% AFP: Alpha-fetoprotein; DCP: Des-gamma-carboxyprothrombin.
[w6567c2-19] AFP-L3 is calculated as a fraction of AFP-L3 to total AFP. The cut-off values most commonly used for a positive AFP-L3 test for patients with HCC is 10% though levels above 15% are more specific[ 33 ]. It is secreted in the initial tumor stages and thus can be used as an early HCC diagnostic marker[ 32 , 34 ]. AFP-L3 level can also distinguish the histological differentiation of tumors[ 25 ]. In patients with total AFP level ≤ 200 ng/mL, AFP-L3 specificity may reach 100% for HCC if it increases more than 35% of the total AFP[ 35 ]. In a large multicenter prospective study of hepatitis C virus related HCC, the specificity of AFP-L3 was observed to be 92% but its sensitivity was fairly low at 37%, irrespective of the tumor stage[ 36 ]. On the contrary, a number of recent studies reported a much higher sensitivity of AFP-L3 for diagnosing HCC[ 37 - 39 ]. Ibrahim et al [ 39 ] evaluated the diagnostic efficacy of AFP-L3 among 20 healthy individuals, 40 patients of chronic active hepatitis and 40 HCC patients with underlying chronic hepatitis B (CHB) or C. Among the HCC patients, 30 (75%) had tumor size > 5 cm. At a cut-off point of > 12.3 ng/mL, AFP-L3 was 100% accurate in diagnosing the HCC[ 39 ]. In a study by Ibrahim et al [ 39 ], 20 healthy individuals, 20 chronic liver disease patients and 40 patients HCV-induced HCC were included in the analysis. A large proportion (65%) of the HCC patients had advanced stage of HCC. By using a cut-off value of 23 ng/mL, AFP-L3 had a sensitivity of 97.5% and specificity of 100% in predicting HCC at[ 37 ]. Cerban et al [ 38 ] investigated the diagnostic performance of AFP, AFP-L3, PIVKA-II and GPC-3 for HCC in a cohort including 52 cirrhotic and 101 HCC patients. AFP-L3 at a cut-off point of > 13.5 ng/mL was found to have a higher sensitivity of 84.7%, compared to other biomarkers[ 38 ]. The sensitivity of AFP-L3 for HCC detection can be affected by its cut-off values, as well as the tumor size and stage[ 40 ]. Data suggested that AFP-L3 could potentially be a valuable prognostic tool. The persistent elevation of AFP-L3 after HCC therapy was found to be associated with shorter survival[ 25 ].
[w6567c2-20] Recently, a highly-sensitive AFP-L3 or hs-AFP-L3 was developed. Toyoda el al. determined the performance of hs-AFP-L3 among patients with 44 patients with chronic hepatitis or cirrhosis, and 54 patients with HCC. The sensitivity and specificity of hs-AFP-L3 were 84.9% and 88.6%, respectively[ 41 ].
[w6567c2-21] Since both total AFP and AFP-L3 have limitations for the detection of HCC, studies have been conducted to combine AFP and AFP-L3 with other novel immunoassays to improve their performance. Des-gamma-carboxyprothrombin (DCP), also known as PIVKA-II (Protein Induced by vitamin K absence or antagonist-II), is a promising HCC biomarker. PIVKA-II is an abnormal product of liver carboxylation that reflects oncogenesis and HCC progression and is undetectable in healthy patients[ 25 , 42 ].
[w6567c2-22] A systematic review noted that AFP, AFP-L3 combined with DCP enhanced their individual HCC predictive values with pooled sensitivity and specificity of 88% and 79%, respectively[ 43 , 44 ]. Since 2002, Japan has included these 3 biomarkers in their HCC surveillance programs and they noted improved rates of early HCC identification and prognosis[ 43 ]. When applying the GALAD score (age, sex, AFP, AFP-L3 and DCP), the HCC diagnostic accuracy was even higher [area under curve (AUC) 0.976][ 45 , 46 ]. A recent meta-analysis reported that AFP combined with GP73 (a Golgi specific membrane protein found in epithelial cells of bile ducts) has favorable sensitivity (84%) and specificity (92%) in diagnosing HCC with an AUROC value of 0.93[ 47 ].
[w6567c2-23] Lately, a new HCC diagnostic marker, the long noncoding RNAs (lncRNAs), was developed[ 48 ]. LncRNAs are transcribed by RNA polymerase II and play a role in the regulation of transcription and translation of proteins. Small extracellular vesicles (EV) transfer proteins, DNA, and RNA between tumor and nontumor cells. The dysregulated lncRNAs including EV-derived lncRNAs contribute to HCC progression and metastasis. EV-derived lncRNAs are, therefore, potential novel serum biomarkers. Kim et al [ 48 ] evaluated LINC00853, an EV-derived lncRNA in 90 patients with HCC and 92 without HCC in a cohort study. The AUROC of LINC00853 was 0.935 for identifying all-stage HCC and 0.969 for detecting early-stage HCC. These results were significantly better compared to AFP[ 48 ]. In another study by Yu et al [ 49 ] reported that the combination of AFP with 2-lncRNA had a higher discriminative power than that of AFP alone in the diagnosis of HCC[ 49 ].
[w6567c2-24] AFP ELEVATION IN LIVER DISEASES AND CIRRHOSIS Rise in AFP has been recorded in different chronic liver diseases without HCC and other malignancies, hence, the results of AFP levels should be interpreted with caution[ 50 ]. AFP is known to have a role in liver regeneration, inflammation and liver fibrosis. Less pronounced AFP elevation > 10 ng/mL has been reported in 15%-58% of chronic hepatitis and 11%-47% of cirrhosis[ 51 ]. The usual cut off for differentiating benign vs malignant liver conditions was at 500 ng/mL but can be variable[ 52 ]. Studies have reported a progressive rise in serum AFP levels with increased histological severity from inflammation to cirrhosis to HCC[ 53 ].
[w6567c2-25] ACUTE HEPATITIS The degree of AFP elevation in acute hepatitis is related to the severity of hepatic destruction[ 50 , 54 ]. The levels range from 10 ng/mL-1000 ng/mL with occasional higher value from 3000 to 7190 ng/mL has been reported. Among children with acute hepatitis B, AFP was detected within 1 wk of the onset of clinical hepatitis and returned to normal by the time of recovery with loss of HBsAg[ 55 ]. The possible mechanisms of AFP elevation include acute phase reaction to the liver injury, hepatocyte regeneration, or viral control or mediated AFP synthesis[ 56 ]. AFP elevation after significant alanine aminotransferase (ALT) elevation is most likely due to liver regeneration and there is generally a latent period of 5-16 d[ 57 ]. AFP generally peaks at a time when liver destruction is subsiding and hepatic remodeling begins[ 57 ]. Hence, the highest level of AFP in acute hepatitis generally occurs during the recovery phase of illness and is an indicator of the liver regenerative process[ 58 ].
[w6567c2-26] CHB Elevated AFP levels are frequently observed during the course of CHB without HCC. The commonest cause of AFP elevation was the exacerbation of the underlying liver disease with or without changes in the status of hepatitis B virus (HBV) replication[ 41 , 52 ]. High AFP levels associated with hepatitis flare were also found to be predictive of HBeAg to anti-HBe seroconversion[ 59 , 60 ]. AFP levels usually decrease within 12 mo on antiviral therapy[ 61 ]. Kim et al [ 61 ] reported that AFP normalization (< 20 ng/mL) was achieved in 89.5% of treated patients but remained abnormal in 40.6% of antiviral treatment naïve patients[ 61 ].
[w6567c2-27] Persistent elevation of AFP > 100 ng/mL without a parallel increase in ALT level was reported to be a predictor of HCC with a sensitivity of 98.7% and specificity of 66.7%[ 59 ]. Yuan et al [ 62 ] conducted a retrospective study of 302 treatment naive CHB patients with AFP positive status. After antiviral therapy, they found that there was a 6.35-fold increased HCC risk among those with persistently elevated AFP compared to those with normalized AFP levels[ 62 ].
[w6567c2-28] In a metanalysis, Peng et al [ 63 ] concluded that among HBV–related HCCs with low AFP levels, circulating miRNAs could be potential valuable biomarkers. For patients with AFP levels < 400 ng/mL, miR-125b and miR-205 demonstrated a sensitivity of 90% while combination of miR-15b and miR-130b had > 90% sensitivity and specificity. For AFP < 20 ng/mL, miR-26a, 27a, 7b and combination of miR-122 and miR-7b exhibited a sensitivity of 80% while combination of mir-29a, 29c, 133a, 143, 145, 192 and 505 produced a specificity of > 80%. Additionally, the combination of miR-15b and miR-130b showed high diagnostic accuracy with sensitivity and specificity exceeding 90%. For differentiating HCC patients with low AFP levels (< 20 ng/mL) from non-HCC patients, the overall sensitivity and specificity of miRNAs were 0.85 and 0.74, respectively with AUC of 0.88 (95%CI: 0.85–0.90). For patients with AFP < 400ng/mL, the overall sensitivity and specificity were 0.84 and 0.76 with AUC of 0.88 (95%CI: 0.84–0.90). Hence, miRNAs are attractive HCC markers to detect HBV-associated HCC with low AFP levels[ 63 ].
[w6567c2-29] Dickkopf WNT signaling pathway inhibitor 1 (DKK-1) is a glycoprotein that is expressed in various malignancies. Shen et al [ 64 ] conducted a study on 424 HCC, 98 CHB, 96 cirrhosis and 213 healthy controls and measured their DKK-1 Levels. The diagnostic cut-off was established at 2.153 ng/mL. DKK-1 proved useful in the detection of AFP-negative HCC with 70.4% sensitivity and 90.0% specificity. These values were validated in another cohort. When combined with AFP, DKK-1 increased detection rate of early-stage HCC with 73.1% sensitivity and 90.0% specificity. Additionally, elevated DKK-1 Levels can help differentiate HCC from other liver diseases such as CHB and cirrhosis with 69.1% sensitivity and 84.7% specificity[ 64 , 65 ].
[w6567c2-30] Hepatitis B flare is defined as an abrupt elevation of serum ALT to 5 times the ULN or greater than 3-fold increase from baseline[ 66 ]. During hepatitis flare, peak AFP level was usually observed 1-2 wk after peak ALT rise. Significant AFP elevation to > 2500 ng/mL had been documented with hepatitis flare. The normalization of AFP might take up to 3-12 mo[ 66 ].
[w6567c2-31] The presentation varies from asymptomatic to overt hepatitis with decompensation and liver failure. AFP elevation was noted in 25%-30% of the patients with hepatitis flares. A study reported the annual incidence of hepatitis flares to be 27% in HBeAg-positive patients and 10% in HBeAg-negative patients with a mean follow-up period of 2 years. Chang et al [ 67 ] reported that patients with AFP > 100 ng/mL during flare cleared HBeAg at a rate of 31% in 3 mo, 62% in 12 mo and 72% in 18 mo. In contrast, the corresponding HBeAg clearance rates for those with AFP < 100 ng/mL were only 4%, 15% and 19% in 3, 12 and 18 mo respectively[ 67 ]. Patients with bridging hepatic necrosis (BHN) had increased activation of the AFP-producing oval cell. BHN was noted in 80% of the cases with AFP levels > 100 mg/dL[ 53 ]. BHN, AFP > 100 ng/mL and decreasing HBV DNA titers were identified as good prognostic indicators of an effective immune control with HBV DNA suppression and/or HBeAg clearance.
[w6567c2-32] Severe and repeated hepatitis flares could also lead to development of cirrhosis or hepatic decompensation. Patients with severe hepatitis flare with BHN or AFP > 100 ng/mL but fail to suppress HBV DNA should be treated promptly with antiviral therapy.
[w6567c2-33] CHRONIC HEPATITIS C AFP elevation is more greatly associated with HCV-associated HCC than HBV-associated HCC[ 64 ]. The incidence of elevated AFP in chronic hepatitis C (CHC) patients range from 10%-43%[ 61 ]. Elevated AFP > 20 ng/dL in CHC is associated with female gender, black race, increased age, genotype 1b, low albumin level, elevated aspartate aminotransferase (AST), elevated AST/ALT ratio, low platelet, prolonged PT, and increased ferritin levels[ 68 , 69 ]. Yang et al [ 70 ] evaluated 279 CHC patients and found no correlation between AFP and the levels of HCV RNA[ 70 ]. In the HALT C (Hepatitis C Antiviral Long-term Treatment) study, Di Bisceglie et al [ 71 ] observed AFP > 20 ng/mL in 16.6% of patients without HCC; it was associated with cirrhosis, female and Black patients[ 71 ]. Black patients with CHC tend to have higher AFP elevation compared to other racial groups[ 71 ]. Generally, HCV-related HCC does not have significant AFP elevation. Thus, AFP has especially low sensitivity to identify HCC in Black populations[ 71 ].
[w6567c2-34] Fouad et al [ 72 ] reported that sustained virological response with direct-acting antiviral agents (DAA) therapy was associated with a significant reduction in serum AFP and might be used as a predictor of treatment response[ 72 ]. In another study, 60% of the DAA treated patients normalized AFP compared to only 23% without therapy[ 61 ]. Therapeutic phlebotomy has also been reported to reduce AFP levels in CHC[ 44 ]. It has been postulated that iron mediated oxidative stress is associated with hepatic injury; iron depletion decreases the oxidative stress and indirectly lowers the AFP level[ 73 ].
[w6567c2-35] NONALCOHOLIC FATTY LIVER DISEASE Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD) has become the most common liver disease in the Western world with about 10%-30% progress to cirrhosis[ 74 ]. It is estimated that 6 million people in the United States have NASH with 1%-2% incidence of HCC annually. Published reports estimated that over 25% of NASH-related HCC occurred in patients without cirrhosis. With the high prevalence of NAFLD, risk stratification is essential to implement HCC surveillance programs[ 75 ]. Currently, no surveillance of NALFD patients for HCC is recommended by AASLD[ 76 ]. However, it has been suggested that NAFLD patients with cirrhosis or possible fibrosis or diabetes mellitus should have 6 monthly surveillances via ultrasound (US) and tumor markers (AFP, AFP-L3 and DCP). Abdominal fat can be a hindrance, hence, patients with obesity can be alternatively surveilled using CT-Scan or magnetic resonance imaging[ 77 ].
[w6567c2-36] An association between AFP elevation and NAFLD was first established by Babalı et al [ 78 ] in 2009. This study demonstrated a significant increase in AFP levels in patients suffering from NAFLD (4.09 ± 1.68) in comparison to the control group (2.95 ± 0.41) with P value < 0.05.Additionally, three subgroups of NAFLD were created based on the liver ultrasonography findings with presence of hepatorenal contrast or bright liver corresponding to grade 1, both hepatorenal contrast and bright liver signifying grade 2, and both pre-mentioned findings with bright liver being severe representing grade 3. The results showed a significant increase in AFP in grade 3 NAFLD (5.43 ± 1.51) in comparison to grade 2 (3.97 ± 1.45) and grade 1 (2.92 ± 1.06) with a P value of 0.001. Triglyceride, cholesterol, low density lipoprotein, high-density lipoprotein, glucose and ALT levels were not significantly different in these groups[ 78 ].
[w6567c2-37] Best et al [ 79 ] published a case control study on German centers in 2020 describing the relationship between HCC and NASH using AFP, AFP-L3, DCP and the GALAD score for assessment. 125 NASH patients with HCC and 231 NASH control patients without HCC were taken. The GALAD score recognized patients with HCC at any stage with an AUC of 0.96 which was superior to AUC of AFP (AUC 0.88), AFP-L3 (AUC 0.86) and DCP (AUC 0.87) levels with P < 0.001 at a cut off about -0.63. The sensitivity was 84.8% and specificity was 95.2% at this cut off. They also conducted Japanese cohort study on 392 patients with NAFLD out of which 28 patients developed HCC. The mean GALAD score in these 28 patients predicted occurrence of HCC 1.5 years before its diagnosis. GALAD score was considerably higher in these patients compared to NASH patients who did not develop HCC[ 79 ].
[w6567c2-38] EARLY VS ADVANCED LIVER CIRRHOSIS WITH DECOMPENSATION Arrieta et al [ 80 ] reported that AFP ≥ 200 and 400 ng/mL had a sensitivity of 36.3% and 20.2% in determining HCC with 100% specificity. Improved method of analysis using progressive elevation of AFP ≥ 7 ng/mL/mo among cirrhotic patients increased the sensitivity to 71.4% with 100% specificity. This method was successful even when AFP values were below 200 ng/mL[ 80 ].
[w6567c2-39] AFP can be elevated in patients with CHC or cirrhosis without any evidence of HCC[ 81 ]. A persistent AFP value of 17.8 ng/mL was noted to have a 98.6% specificity, 35% sensitivity and a positive predictive value of 97.7% in identifying patients with cirrhosis[ 82 ]. Sudden fluctuations in AFP levels in patients with cirrhosis can be indicative of hepatitis flare, deterioration of liver disease and development of HCC[ 83 ]. AFP has been applied routinely for HCC surveillance in cirrhotic patients. Harada et al [ 83 ] showed that 40% of cirrhotic patients had AFP levels > 20 mg/dL[ 83 ]. Manuc et al [ 84 ] reported in a study of 2068 CHC patients with cirrhosis, 30.1% without HCC had AFP > 15 ng/mL due to advanced age, severe liver injury with raised AST and ALT levels and low platelets[ 84 ].
[w6567c2-40] In patients with cirrhosis, both the sensitivity and specificity of AFP for detection of early-stage HCC diagnosis can be enhanced by combining it with US. In a meta-analysis, the pooled sensitivity of AFP and US for any stage and early-stage HCC were 95% (83%-100%) and 60% (45%–74%) respectively, compared to only 72% (56%–86%) and 40% (22%–58%) for US only[ 85 ]. Surveillance with AFP and ultrasonography in cirrhotic patients having an annual risk of HCC > 0.4% is cost-effective[ 86 ].
[w6567c2-41] There are many risk scores that have been developed to predict HCC development among patients with chronic hepatitis and cirrhosis. However, many of them have not been standardized or carefully validated in different patient populations. Ideally, HCC prediction models should be developed for treated and untreated patients taking into account the patient ethnicity, significant comorbid conditions and ideally the molecular biomarkers[ 87 ].
[w6567c2-42] PERSISTENT AFP ELEVATION IN THE ABSENCE OF LIVER DISEASE AFP is an important screening test for HCC and is also used in the diagnostic evaluation of other hepatic and non-hepatic conditions. Persistent AFP elevation has also been reported in patients without malignant or nonmalignant conditions. Hereditary persistence of AFP (HPAFP), a benign autosomal dominant disorder with no apparent disease or abnormality, should be considered as one of the differential diagnoses in patients with unclear etiology of persistent AFP elevation[ 88 ]. HPAFP is a rare condition with only 20 reported cases in the literature[ 89 ]. In contrast to malignant tumors with AFP usually > 500 ng/mL, the AFP concentration in HPAFP is mostly below 200 ng/mL, but levels up to 1500 ng/mL have been reported in some cases[ 88 , 90 , 91 ]. The molecular mechanism of HPAFP can differ in unrelated families. Specific heterozygous point mutations are frequently found in the promoter region of the AFP gene related to hepatocyte nuclear factor 1 (HNF1) binding sites[ 92 , 93 ]. These mutations usually result in an increased affinity for HNF1 and subsequently lead to increased AFP promoter activity and AFP gene transcription[ 94 ]. Two-point mutations have been identified; the upstream substitution of cytosine (C) with adenosine (A) at amino acid position of 55 (-55 C > A) in the proximal HNF-1 binding site and upstream mutation caused by the substitution of Guanine (G) by Adenosine (A) at position 119 (-119 G > A) in the distal HNF-1 binding site[ 95 , 96 ]. The first case of HPAFP was reported in 1984 by Greenberg et al [ 91 ] in a Scottish family where a 38-year-old woman was noted to have persistently elevated AFP during post-partum[ 91 ]. The AFP concentration of her amniotic fluid was normal and HPAFP was later confirmed by evaluating serum AFP levels and molecular testing in family members. In another report of 20 HPAFP cases, 2 patients underwent unnecessary surgery and 3 had unnecessary chemotherapy due to their persistently elevated AFP[ 96 ]. Though rare, hereditary causes should be considered in patients with unexplained and persistent AFP elevation. In a recent study by Jeon et al [ 89 ] on 4 Korean patients with persistently elevated AFP levels from 12.1 to 186.1 ng/mL for > 1 year, 1 patient was found to have a hereditary cause by pedigree analysis even though the typical mutation of the AFP gene in the promoter region was absent. This case elucidated the heterogeneous nature of persistent AFP elevation and HPAFP is not always the result of mutation in the AFP transcription regulatory regions[ 89 ].
[w6567c2-43] CONCLUSION AFP has been used for HCC surveillance widely in clinical practice. It, however, has limited sensitivity and specificity for HCC detection, and a proportion of patients with advanced HCC do not have AFP secretion. Moreover, patients with chronic liver diseases, especially those with cirrhosis are commonly identified with persistent AFP elevation without radiological evidence of HCC. For all these reasons, AFP is not recommended to be used as a sole marker alone for HCC surveillance. The diagnostic potential of AFP for early diagnosis of HCC can be enhanced by combining it with other novel diagnostic markers such as AFP-L3, PIVKA-II, and GP73. Although many have already endorsed diagnostic value, large number of multicenter studies encompassing larger cohorts and long-term assessment are required to confirm clinical utility. Additionally, algorithms integrating these serum markers with noninvasive diagnostic imaging modalities are needed to be developed for the early and accurate diagnosis of HCC.

[w6567c2-45] Figure 1 ELISA measures. A: ELISA measures alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) by detecting reactivity with two anti-AFP antibodies; B: Interfering antibodies that bind directly to the capture and detect reagents without the target AFP (no analytes). This binding would emit light and become luminescent causing false positive results and high values; C: Interfering antibodies can inhibit reactivity of the ELISA by binding to the reagents and prevent the detection of AFP by the anti-AFP antibodies; that leads to a false negative result. AFP: Alpha-fetoprotein.

[w2dc485-1] J Clin Exp Hepatol J Clin Exp Hepatol 2306 jceh 101574137 Journal of Clinical and Experimental Hepatology 0973-6883 2213-3453 Elsevier PMC4284240 PMC4284240.1 4284240 4284240 25755615 10.1016/j.jceh.2014.03.045 S0973-6883(14)00222-9 1 Review Article Staging of Hepatocellular Carcinoma Duseja Ajay ajayduseja@yahoo.co.in ∗ Department of Hepatology, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh 160012, India ∗ Address for correspondence : Department of Hepatology, Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh 160012, India. Tel.: +91 (0) 172 2756336; fax: +91 (0) 172 2744401. ajayduseja@yahoo.co.in 8 2014 06 6 2014 4 Suppl 3 247248 S74 S79 22 8 2013 3 3 2014 06 06 2014 09 03 2015 04 07 2023 © 2014, INASL. 2014 Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is different from other malignancies because the prognosis in HCC is not only dependent upon the tumor stage but also on the liver function impairment due to accompanying cirrhosis liver. Various other staging systems used in HCC include the European systems [French staging system, Barcelona Clinic Liver Cancer (BCLC) staging system and the cancer of the liver Italian program (CLIP)] and Asian systems [Okuda staging system, Japan integrated Staging (JIS), Tokyo score and Chinese University Prognostic Index (CUPI)]. Out of all the staging systems used in HCC, Barcelona Clinic Liver Cancer (BCLC) staging system is probably the best because it takes in to account the tumor status (defined by tumor size and number, presence of vascular invasion and extrahepatic spread), liver function (defined either by the Child-Pugh’s class) and general health status of the patient (defined by the ECOG classification and the presence of symptoms). Since most of the extrahepatic spread in HCC occurs to lymph nodes, lungs and bones, the assessment can be done with either PET/CT or a combination of CT (Chest and abdomen) and a bone scan. This article describes the various staging systems used in HCC, guides choosing a staging system particularly in the Indian context and the assessment of extra-hepatic spread in HCC.

[w7bd853-3] 15 3 2003 15 3 2003 9 3 259874 385 391 9 10 2002 23 10 2002 25 11 2002 15 03 2003 28 10 2015 29 10 2015 ©The Author(s) 2003. Published by Baishideng Publishing Group Inc. All rights reserved. 2003 Obstructive jaundice as the main clinical feature is uncommon in patients with hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC). Only 1%-12% of HCC patients manifest obstructive jaundice as the initial complaint. Such cases are clinically classified as “icteric type hepatoma”, or “cholestatic type of HCC”. Identification of this group of patients is important, because surgical treatment may be beneficial. HCC may involve the biliary tract in several different ways: tumor thrombosis, hemobilia, tumor compression, and diffuse tumor infiltration. Bile duct thrombosis (BDT) is one of the main causes for obstructive jaundice, and the previously reported incidence is 1.2%-9%. BDT might be benign, malignant, or a combination of both. Benign thrombi could be blood clots, pus, or sludge. Malignant thrombi could be primary intrabiliary malignant tumors, HCC with invasion to bile ducts, or metastatic cancer with bile duct invasion. The common clinical features of this type of HCC include: high level of serum AFP; history of cholangitis with dilation of intrahepatic bile duct; aggravating jaundice and rapidly developing into liver dysfunction. It is usually difficult to make diagnosis before operation, because of the low incidence rate, ignorant of this disease, and the difficulty for the imaging diagnosis to find the BDT preoperatively. Despite recent remarkable improvements in the imaging tools for diagnosis of HCC, such cases are still incorrectly diagnosed as cholangiocarcinoma or choledocholithiases. Ultrasonography (US) and CT are helpful in showing hepatic tumors and dilated intrahepatic and /or extrahepatic ducts containing dense material corresponding to tumor debris. Direct cholangiography including percutaneous transhepatic cholangiography (PTC) and endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography (ERCP) remains the standard procedure to delineate the presence and level of biliary obstruction. Magnetic resonance cholangiopancreatography (MRCP) is superior to ERCP in interpreting the cause and depicting the anatomical extent of the perihilar obstructive jaundice, and is particularly distinctive in cases associated with tight biliary stenosis and along segmental biliary stricture. Choledochoscopy and bile duct brushing cytology could be alternative useful techniques in the differentiating obstructions due to intraluminal mass, infiltrating ductal lesions or extrinsic mass compression applicable before and after duct exploration. Jaundice is not necessarily a contraindication for surgery. Most patients will have satisfactory palliation and occasional cure if appropriate procedures are selected and carried out safely, which can result in long-term resolution of symptoms and occasional long-term survival. However, the prognosis of icteric type HCC is generally dismal, but is better than those HCC patients who have jaundice caused by hepatic insufficiency. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access no pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes
